### Warehouse 13

**Classification:** Exceptional closest-match candidate

**Confidence:** Very high

**Concept prominence:** 93.5  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 90.7  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**Nearest verified vibe:** Charmed (2018) · 87.1

Warehouse 13 is unusually compassion-dense because its procedural engine often depends on recognizing that the person causing harm is being influenced by an artifact, traumatized by loss or driven by a grievance that can be separated from the dangerous object. The agents' job is therefore commonly containment plus rescue, not simply defeating a villain. Over time the Warehouse team becomes a strong multigenerational found family.

**Why it matches:** It is a direct Good Witch/Legacies hybrid in structure: episodic helping is constant, but within a dangerous supernatural world. The team's recurring move is to look beyond bizarre behavior to the artifact, grief or history underneath it. H.G. Wells and Claudia provide especially strong second-chance arcs.

#### Quantitative occurrence estimate

- Estimated distinct compassion occurrences: **~312**
- Estimated compassion-focused screen time: **~936 minutes**
- Estimated share of total runtime: **34%**
- Estimated occurrences per screen hour: **6.8**

#### Major findings

- **Artifact influence complicates blame** — The show repeatedly distinguishes what someone chose from what an artifact amplified, compelled or distorted.
- **The procedural goal is often rescue plus containment** — Agents usually seek to neutralize the artifact without treating the affected person as disposable.
- **Claudia enters as antagonist and becomes family** — Her initial hacking/kidnapping is rooted in desperate belief that her brother can be saved; understanding motive changes Artie's response and opens a long-term place for her.
- **H.G. Wells is allowed a difficult redemption arc** — She attempts catastrophic destruction out of grief, betrays trust and later repeatedly risks herself to protect the team, making redemption active rather than declarative.
- **Myka's willingness to trust H.G. is morally consequential** — Her belief that H.G. can be more than her worst act helps preserve the possibility of later repair, even when that trust is painful.
- **Steve Jinks is valued beyond his lie-detection ability** — His truth-sensing power causes isolation; the team gradually becomes a place where he is cared for as a person rather than used as an instrument.
- **Resurrection creates ethical debt** — Claudia's use of the metronome to bring Steve back transfers his pain to her, turning a loving rescue into an autonomy/cost problem the series takes seriously.
- **Artie and Claudia form a parent-child bond** — Their relationship includes control, rebellion, fear and mutual sacrifice; Artie's care becomes increasingly personal rather than managerial.
- **Grief repeatedly creates dangerous choices** — MacPherson, H.G., Artie, Claudia and others make catastrophic decisions around loss. Understanding grief helps explain behavior without making outcomes harmless.
- **The Warehouse itself is not morally infallible** — Regents, Caretakers and procedures can be secretive or coercive; agents sometimes have to choose people over institutional logic.
- **Claire's condition is treated as something to heal, not moral corruption** — Claudia's sister becomes another example of separating a dangerous altered state from the person's underlying worth.
- **Team members remain family after betrayal** — Possession, artifact influence, secret plans and even major treachery do not automatically end relationship; repair is repeatedly attempted.
- **Pete's empathy is practical, not merely comic** — His emotional sensitivity often lets him notice fear, grief or attachment before a purely procedural approach would.
- **Ordinary domestic care keeps the team functional** — Meals, teasing, checking injuries, worrying about sleep and remembering family needs are unusually frequent for a procedural adventure.
- **The finale defines the Warehouse through people** — The institution matters because of the relationships and lives formed around it, not merely because it stores powerful objects.

#### Complications / limits

- **Artifact causation can reduce accountability too much** — When magical influence explains behavior, the story can sidestep harder questions about what the person already wanted or chose.
- **The Warehouse is paternalistic** — Secrecy, recruitment and containment often occur without full consent, even when framed as protecting the world.
- **Main characters receive exceptional chances** — Recurring agents and allies are rescued or forgiven in situations where episodic antagonists may receive less patience.
- **Resurrection and artifact fixes can soften irreversible consequence** — The supernatural toolkit occasionally makes loss more reversible than the show's grief themes would otherwise imply.

#### Character compassion arcs

- **Claudia Donovan** — Begins as desperate hacker trying to save her brother and becomes the team's emotional/technical daughter figure, future Caretaker candidate and fierce rescuer of others.
- **Artie Nielsen** — A secretive, controlling mentor whose care for Claudia, Pete, Myka and Steve gradually becomes explicit family attachment.
- **Myka Bering** — Often provides the team's most reflective moral judgment and is especially willing to see personhood and redeemability where others see risk.
- **Pete Lattimer** — Uses instinct, humor and emotional openness to keep cases person-centered; his sensitivity repeatedly helps the team identify hidden human stakes.
- **H.G. Wells** — Moves from brilliant estranged agent through grief-driven attempted apocalypse toward repeated protective sacrifice and renewed trust.
- **Steve Jinks** — A man whose ability to detect lies alienates him becomes a trusted family member, dies, is resurrected through Claudia's refusal to let him go, and then confronts the cost of that rescue.
- **Claire Donovan** — Her dangerous altered condition becomes a family-care problem rather than evidence she should be discarded.

#### Representative transcript / scene evidence

- **1x04 Claudia**
  - Claudia initially appears as hostile hacker/kidnapper.
  - Her actions are rooted in refusal to abandon her brother Joshua, whom everyone else believes lost.
  - Artie's understanding of motive changes the response from punishment toward help and eventual belonging.
  - *Functions:* context before condemnation, family rescue, second chance
- **1x11 Nevermore**
  - Myka's abusive/controlling family history becomes relevant to how she responds to danger and authority.
  - The episode makes personal history part of effective care rather than treating agents as interchangeable professionals.
  - *Functions:* family trauma, empathy, support
- **2x10 Where and When**
  - H.G.'s murdered daughter is established as central to her grief and later choices.
  - The team gives her structured reintegration despite Artie's distrust.
  - The episode preserves both caution and possibility of change.
  - *Functions:* grief context, reintegration, conditional trust
- **2x12 Reset**
  - H.G.'s betrayal becomes catastrophic, but Myka's emotional response includes grief over having believed in her rather than simple hatred.
  - Later seasons build redemption from this rupture rather than denying it happened.
  - *Functions:* betrayal, complex mercy, redemption groundwork
- **3x01 The New Guy**
  - Myka withdraws because she believes her trust nearly destroyed the world.
  - The team does not treat her as permanently disqualified; Steve's arrival creates a new relational configuration rather than replacement as punishment.
  - *Functions:* self-forgiveness, team belonging, newcomer inclusion
- **3x11 Emily Lake**
  - Steve's death produces raw grief in Claudia while Myka suppresses her own reaction to keep functioning.
  - The team makes room for different grief responses rather than declaring one correct.
  - *Functions:* grief care, emotional difference
- **4x06 Fractures**
  - Steve worries that Claudia is literally suffering his pain because she resurrected him.
  - He seeks a way to free her from the cost even if it endangers him, turning gratitude into reciprocal care.
  - *Functions:* autonomy, reciprocal sacrifice, resurrection ethics
- **5x04 Savage Seduction**
  - Artie recognizes Claudia's obsessive search around her sister is unhealthy and tries to interrupt it through connection and work rather than punishment.
  - Family care becomes concern about when helping has turned into self-destructive fixation.
  - *Functions:* caregiver intervention, family concern
- **series-wide artifact cases**
  - Many antagonistic behaviors are caused or amplified by artifacts.
  - Agents repeatedly try to neutralize the object and save the person, making compassion part of the procedural structure.
  - *Functions:* restorative intervention, personhood beyond altered behavior
- **5x06 Endless**
  - The finale frames Warehouse history around the people and relationships formed there.
  - Belonging and memory, rather than artifacts alone, become the institution's emotional legacy.
  - *Functions:* found family, institutional meaning

### Wynonna Earp

**Classification:** Very strong closest-match candidate

**Confidence:** Moderate

**Concept prominence:** 80.7  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 79.8  
**Vibe class:** STRONG NEIGHBORHOOD MATCH  
**Nearest verified vibe:** Legacies · 85.1

Wynonna Earp is less consistently anti-monster-essentialist than Charmed or Legacies because many revenants remain enemies to be killed, but its major character arcs are deeply compassion-driven. The Earp family repeatedly treats love as stronger than curse, bloodline, supernatural origin or moral failure. Wynonna's hardest choices often involve protecting someone she loves at severe personal cost, while Doc, Waverly, Nicole, Jeremy and even some supernatural figures complicate the idea that demon, vampire, angelic or cursed identity determines moral worth.

**Why it matches:** Its best matches are found family, fierce protection, redemption after severe harm, queer relational care, and refusal to let supernatural identity wholly define a person. It is particularly close to Legacies in its 'dangerous family member is still family' ethic, but less universal in compassion toward one-off monsters.

#### Quantitative occurrence estimate

- Estimated distinct compassion occurrences: **~169**
- Estimated compassion-focused screen time: **~590 minutes**
- Estimated share of total runtime: **28%**
- Estimated occurrences per screen hour: **4.8**

#### Major findings

- **The Earp curse does not define Wynonna's worth** — Wynonna returns carrying shame, family rejection and a violent inherited role. The series repeatedly separates what she is expected to be from the person she chooses to become.
- **Waverly's non-Earp origin does not weaken family** — Discoveries about Waverly's parentage and supernatural nature repeatedly test whether biology defines sisterhood; Wynonna's answer is consistently no.
- **Protecting Alice requires painful separation** — Wynonna gives up immediate motherhood because keeping Alice near the supernatural conflict would endanger her, treating love as willingness to endure loss for the child's safety.
- **Nicole and Waverly model care through crisis** — Their relationship survives possession, memory alteration, injury, supernatural identity revelations and separation through explicit reassurance and practical rescue.
- **Doc's humanity is morally complicated, not fixed** — Immortality and later vampirism alter him but do not make him automatically evil; the series judges his choices, loyalties and willingness to repair.
- **Bobo is more than a demon-category villain** — His long history with Waverly and protective impulses complicate the revenant category, even though he remains dangerous and culpable.
- **Michelle distinguishes demons from moral essence** — Her warning that some demons can be 'sweet' while some men are vicious directly articulates the show's rejection of simple human/demon moral binaries.
- **Wynonna's protection often includes people she is angry with** — She repeatedly rescues or defends family and allies during relational rupture rather than requiring emotional resolution first.
- **Grief is converted into responsibility rather than only revenge** — Losses often intensify Wynonna's anger, but the durable arc is keeping remaining people alive rather than simply retaliating.
- **Jeremy becomes family through vulnerability** — His place in the group develops from institutional role toward trusted emotional belonging despite fear, secrecy and later power structures.
- **Nedley offers ordinary protective community** — His loyalty to Wynonna and Purgatory often takes the form of shelter, practical backup and belief before full explanation is available.
- **Supernatural identity can be discovered rather than chosen** — Waverly, Doc and others become examples of people having to integrate identities they did not consent to or fully understand.
- **Love does not erase boundaries** — Wynonna and others sometimes forgive or continue loving people they still refuse to trust fully, preserving a useful distinction between care and moral blank check.
- **Found family repeatedly outperforms institutions** — Black Badge and other formal systems are often coercive or instrumental; the group survives because personal loyalty fills ethical gaps institutions leave behind.

#### Complications / limits

- **Revenants are often treated categorically** — The curse structure encourages a kill-or-be-killed approach that gives many supernatural opponents little room for personhood.
- **Intimate loyalty can overshadow wider moral consistency** — Main characters receive more contextual compassion than anonymous enemies.
- **Violence is central to resolution** — Mercy and understanding exist, but Peacemaker and lethal force remain the default for many threats.
- **Transcript confidence is lower** — A full searchable subtitle/transcript corpus was not located, so this audit is more dependent on detailed summaries and character histories than the strongest entries.

#### Character compassion arcs

- **Wynonna Earp** — Moves from shame and self-protective cynicism toward a fierce protector who accepts being loved and repeatedly chooses others' safety over her own comfort.
- **Waverly Earp** — Her identity becomes increasingly supernatural and uncertain, but family and romantic love repeatedly affirm that belonging does not depend on origin.
- **Nicole Haught** — Functions as one of the series' most stable care figures: practical protection, emotional reassurance and willingness to remain beside Waverly through supernatural change.
- **Doc Holliday** — Moves through selfishness, loyalty, fatherhood, vampirism and mortality while repeatedly confronting whether love can make him choose differently from the survivor he used to be.
- **Jeremy Chetri** — An anxious outsider whose competence and vulnerability gradually become integral to the family network.
- **Michelle Gibson** — Her history demonstrates that protecting family from supernatural danger can require choices others interpret as abandonment or madness.

#### Representative transcript / scene evidence

- **2x10 I See a Darkness**
  - Nicole's life is endangered and Waverly's fear drives desperate choices.
  - The crisis shows how love can become coercive or reckless when losing someone feels unbearable.
  - *Functions:* protective desperation, relationship care, ethical tension
- **2x11 Gone as a Girl Can Get**
  - A world where Wynonna is erased leaves Waverly disconnected from key parts of herself.
  - Doc helps Waverly remember, and recognition of Wynonna restores both family memory and agency.
  - *Functions:* family identity, memory, rescue
- **2x12 I Hope You Dance**
  - Waverly helps Wynonna give birth under supernatural attack.
  - Wynonna entrusts Alice to safety even though giving her up is devastating.
  - She insists Doc be allowed a moment with his daughter, preserving his relationship despite their conflict.
  - *Functions:* maternal sacrifice, sister care, family inclusion
- **3x05 Jolene**
  - Waverly is targeted through a supernatural force exploiting loneliness and insecurity.
  - Her relationships become the counterweight to a narrative telling her she is unwanted.
  - *Functions:* anti-isolation, belonging, emotional rescue
- **season 3 Michelle arc**
  - Wynonna confronts her mother's absence and discovers protective motives entangled with trauma and supernatural danger.
  - New context changes abandonment into a more morally complicated story without eliminating hurt.
  - *Functions:* context before judgment, family repair
- **4x01 On the Road Again**
  - Doc enters the Garden to retrieve Waverly rather than leave her trapped by supernatural forces.
  - The rescue continues the series' pattern that family obligations persist across species/state changes.
  - *Functions:* rescue, family loyalty
- **4x12 Old Souls**
  - The finale emphasizes choosing a future rather than remaining trapped in curse-driven identity.
  - Wynonna and Doc move toward relationship after repeated harm and separation; Waverly and Nicole formalize durable chosen family.
  - *Functions:* repair, hope, found family
- **series-wide Waverly/Nicole arc**
  - Possession, injury, supernatural origin and memory disruption repeatedly fail to make either partner disposable.
  - Care is expressed through staying, believing and rescuing without requiring the other to remain unchanged.
  - *Functions:* queer found family, personhood through change
- **series-wide demon/human ambiguity**
  - Bobo, Doc, Waverly and Michelle's dialogue complicate the assumption that supernatural category determines moral worth.
  - The principle is meaningful but inconsistently extended to all revenants.
  - *Functions:* personhood over category, anti-essentialism

#### Compassion dimension vector

| Dimension | Score |
|---|---:|
| Compassion is treated as morally consequential rather than merely pleasant. | 5/5 |
| Empathy or mercy changes what characters actually do. | 5/5 |
| Characters repeatedly protect vulnerable people/beings. | 5/5 |
| Characters seek nonlethal/restorative alternatives where destruction is easier. | 3/5 |
| Species, supernatural type, social label, or role does not determine moral worth. | 4/5 |
| Compassion extends to monsters, spirits, aliens, artificial beings, animals, enemies, or stigmatized outsiders. | 4/5 |
| The work resists inherited guilt, guilt by association, or essentialist blame. | 4/5 |
| Morally compromised characters are allowed meaningful possibility of repair. | 5/5 |
| Care creates durable kinship beyond conventional family boundaries. | 5/5 |
| The work takes grief seriously and answers it with care rather than only vengeance. | 5/5 |
| Characters accept meaningful cost for another's welfare. | 5/5 |
| Compassion extends beyond one intimate relationship to a group/community. | 4/5 |
| The story challenges systems or rules that produce dehumanization or unjust harm. | 3/5 |
| Compassion includes respect for agency, memory, consent, and self-determination. | 4/5 |
| Small everyday acts of care recur frequently. | 3/5 |
| Characters try to understand motives, pain, or alien perspectives rather than simply pitying. | 4/5 |
| The compassion operates under meaningful threat, grief, violence, or moral pressure. | 5/5 |
| Humor may coexist with serious compassion rather than trivializing it. | 4/5 |
| Helping someone is a repeated structural engine of episodes. | 4/5 |
| Nonhuman life is treated as morally significant without requiring humanlike motives. | 1/5 |

## Data note

Occurrence and screen-time values are modeled estimates derived from transcript/subtitle/screenplay audits, not stopwatch measurements. Theme minutes overlap and must not be summed as if mutually exclusive.
